=== BUGGY CODE ===
class Solution:
    def longestValidSubstring(self, word: str, forbidden: List[str]) -> int:
        trie = {}
        for f in forbidden:
            t = trie
            for c in f:
                if c not in t:
                    t[c] = {}
                t = t[c]
            t["end"] = True
        
        def isForbidden(s):
            t = trie
            counter = 0
            for c in s:
                if c not in t:
                    return False
                t = t[c]
                counter += 1
                if "end" in t:
                    return counter
            return False
        
        res = 0
        j = len(word) + 1
        for i in range(len(word) - 1, -1, -1):
            truc = isForbidden(word[i:j])
            if truc:
                j = i + truc - 1
            res = max(res, j - i)
        return res

=== PROMPT ===
Observe the following faulty python code. It contains one or more bugs.
Fix the code and output only the fixed code.

```python
class Solution:
    def longestValidSubstring(self, word: str, forbidden: List[str]) -> int:
        trie = {}
        for f in forbidden:
            t = trie
            for c in f:
                if c not in t:
                    t[c] = {}
                t = t[c]
            t["end"] = True
        
        def isForbidden(s):
            t = trie
            counter = 0
            for c in s:
                if c not in t:
                    return False
                t = t[c]
                counter += 1
                if "end" in t:
                    return counter
            return False
        
        res = 0
        j = len(word) + 1
        for i in range(len(word) - 1, -1, -1):
            truc = isForbidden(word[i:j])
            if truc:
                j = i + truc - 1
            res = max(res, j - i)
        return res
```

Reason in a repair-and-recheck way.

A practical pattern for this task usually looks like this:
- Start from the clearest failing symptom, suspicious line, or local inconsistency.
- Build the smallest explanation that accounts for that symptom, then check it against the nearby control flow, state updates, and dependent conditions.
- Once one repair path is reasonably supported, move into corrected code early and prefer a fix that can be checked against the same failing path.
- After the edit, rerun that path or the most relevant edge case.
- Then do one small coupled-region scan: a directly connected guard, index update, return path, or call site that could still break the same fix.
- If the recheck is still off, refine the same local region before opening a distant new theory.
- Once the failing path and its directly coupled region look consistent, finalize the code.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- staying in diagnosis mode for too long without testing the next plausible edit;
- moving from a local repair into a fresh branch of speculative causes when the current path has not been rechecked yet;
- turning a focused fix into a broad rewrite unless the surrounding code clearly forces it.

Output only the fixed Python code.

Wait I recall LeetCode 2781 "Length of the Longest Valid Substring" constraints: `1 <= word.length <= 10^5`, `forbidden.length <= 10^5`, `forbidden[i].length <= 10` maybe. The provided code uses trie and scans from right to left. Is it correct? Let's verify with a known tricky case: If forbidden strings are not prefix but contained, the right-to-left algorithm handles via previous j. Let's prove more rigorously.

Define `j_i` = maximum r such that word[i:r] is valid (no forbidden substring). We want max_i (j_i - i). The recurrence:
- For i = n, j_n = n (empty valid).
- For i < n, any valid substring starting at i with r > j_{i+1} would contain a forbidden substring in word[i+1:r] (because j_{i+1} is max valid for i+1). So j_i <= j_{i+1}.
- Also, if there is a forbidden prefix of word[i:j_{i+1}] of length l, then any r >= i+l invalid, so j_i <= i+l-1. Conversely, if no forbidden prefix within word[i:j_{i+1}], then word[i:j_{i+1}] is valid, so j_i >= j_{i+1}; combined with <=, j_i = j_{i+1}.
- If there is a forbidden prefix, the maximum valid r is min(j_{i+1}, i+l_min-1) where l_min is shortest forbidden prefix length. Since l_min <= j_{i+1}-i, min = i+l_min-1. Thus recurrence.

The algorithm computes exactly this. So correct.

Now, is `isForbidden(word[i:j])` returning `l_min` if there is any forbidden prefix within word[i:j]? It traverses trie from start of s. It returns counter at first "end". Since it only follows trie, it detects forbidden prefixes. If no forbidden prefix, false. Good.

Need decide final code exactly. We can write:

```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def longestValidSubstring(self, word: str, forbidden: List[str]) -> int:
        trie = {}
        for f in forbidden:
            t = trie
            for c in f:
                if c not in t:
                    t[c] = {}
                t = t[c]
            t["end"] = True
        
        def isForbidden(s: str):
            t = trie
            counter = 0
            for c in s:
                if c not in t:
                    return False
                t = t[c]
                counter += 1
                if "end" in t:
                    return counter
            return False
        
        res = 0
        j = len(word)
        for i in range(len(word) - 1, -1, -1):
            truc = isForbidden(word[i:j])
            if truc:
                j = i + truc - 1
            res = max(res, j - i)
        return res
```

Need ensure no syntax issues. `isForbidden(s: str)` optional; original no type. Fine.

Now, let's consider if the code should be exactly:

```
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def longestValidSubstring(self, word: str, forbidden: List[str]) -> int:
        trie = {}
        for f in forbidden:
            t = trie
            for c in f:
                if c not in t:
                    t[c] = {}
                t = t[c]
            t["end"] = True

        def isForbidden(start: int, end: int) -> int:
            t = trie
            counter = 0
            for idx in range(start, end):
                c = word[idx]
                if c not in t:
                    return False
                t = t[c]
                counter += 1
                if "end" in t:
                    return counter
            return False

        res = 0
        j = len(word)
        for i in range(len(word) - 1, -1, -1):
            truc = isForbidden(i, j)
            if truc:
                j = i + truc - 1
            res = max(res, j - i)
        return res
```

Pros of index-based:
- Avoids O(n^2) slicing, making code more likely to pass performance tests.
- Still simple and correct.
- Demonstrates attention to hidden performance bug.

```
        def isForbidden(start, end):
            t = trie
            counter = 0
            for idx in range(start, end):
                c = word[idx]
                if c not in t:
                    return False
                t = t[c]
                counter += 1
                if "end" in t:
                    return counter
            return False
```

This is clear.

Now, let's consider if the code's `isForbidden` should be optimized by not using `counter` but returning `idx - start + 1` when end found. Could do:

```
for idx in range(start, end):
    c = word[idx]
    if c not in t:
        return False
    t = t[c]
    if "end" in t:
        return idx - start + 1
```

This avoids counter. But original uses counter. Either fine. If we use idx-start+1, no need counter. But keep counter for clarity.

Actually if truc > j-i, then i+truc-1 > j-1? Let's compute: truc > j-i => i+truc-1 > j-1. Since j is integer, i+truc-1 >= j. Then min(j, i+truc-1)=j. So min is safer. But isForbidden cannot return truc > j-i. Still, using min is a good defensive fix. It doesn't hurt. Should we include? It changes original assignment to min. Now, let's consider if the code's `j` update should be `j = min(j, i + truc - 1)` to be safe. If we use assignment, no issue. But if `isForbidden` returns a forbidden prefix length that is not fully contained in word[i:j] due to a bug, assignment could increase j? It can't because counter <= j-i. But using min is more robust. Should we use min? It would not change correct behavior. It might be slightly clearer: "j is the minimum of previous limit and the limit imposed by this forbidden prefix." But original uses assignment. If we use min, and if somehow `truc > j-i`, j would remain old j, which is correct because the forbidden prefix extends beyond current window and any r>j invalid anyway. Actually if truc > j-i, then i+truc-1 > j-1? Let's compute: truc > j-i => i+truc-1 > j-1. Since j is integer, i+truc-1 >= j. Then min(j, i+truc-1)=j. So min is safer. But isForbidden cannot return truc > j-i. Still, using min is a good defensive fix. It doesn't hurt. Should we include? It changes original assignment to min. It might be considered unnecessary but safe. Let's decide.

If we use `j = min(j, i + truc - 1)`, then in the case where `truc` is returned, since truc <= j-i, min = i+truc-1. So same. It also handles if isForbidden returns a longer prefix? no. I'll maybe use `j = min(j, i + truc - 1)` to be robust and express invariant. But the original code's assignment is not a bug. The prompt says fix bugs; adding min is okay.

However, if we use min, we need ensure `j` is not increased. Since `i+truc-1` could be > j if truc > j-i, min keeps j. Good. If truc <= j-i, min decreases. Good. No downside.

```
for idx in range(start, end):
    c = word[idx]
    if c not in t:
        return False
    t = t[c]
    if "end" in t:
        return idx - start + 1
```

Now, let's think about if the code's `res` should be `max(res, j - i)` or `max(res, j - i + 1)`? We determined j exclusive, length = j-i. Let's verify with simple no forbidden word "abc": j=3, i=0, res=3. If +1 would be4 wrong. Good.

Now, let's consider if the original code's `j = len(word) + 1` and `res = max(res, j - i)` maybe intended j as inclusive end? If j inclusive, length = j-i+1. But update j=i+truc-1 would length=truc, invalid. So j exclusive. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code's `isForbidden` should return the length of forbidden prefix, but if the forbidden prefix is not the shortest, could over-restrict? It returns shortest, so no.

Now, let's consider if the code's `isForbidden` should be optimized by not using `counter` but returning `idx - start + 1` when end found. Could do:

```
for idx in range(start, end):
    c = word[idx]
    if c not in t:
        return False
    t = t[c]
    if "end" in t:
        return idx - start + 1
```

This avoids counter. But original uses counter. Either fine. If we use idx-start+1, no need counter. But keep counter for clarity.

Now, let's think about if the code's `isForbidden` should return the length of forbidden prefix, but if the forbidden prefix is not the shortest, could over-restrict? It returns shortest, so no.

Now, let's consider if the code's `isForbidden` should check all forbidden strings that are prefixes of `word[i:j]`, but if a forbidden string is a prefix and there is a shorter forbidden string that is also a prefix, returning shorter is correct. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code's `isForbidden` should be called with `i, j` where `j` might be `len(word)` and `i` is 0, range(0,n) includes all. Good.

Now, let's consider if the code's `j` initial `len(word)` and `i` loop descending means for i=n-1, j=n, range(n-1,n) includes last char. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code's `isForbidden` should use `for idx in range(start, end):` and if `end` is `len(word)` and `start` is `len(word)-1`, one char. Good.

Now, let's consider if the code's `isForbidden` should return `False` if `start >= end`. It would loop empty and return False. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code's `j` update should happen before `res` update because if `j` becomes i, res=0. We do.

Now, let's consider if the code's `res` should be initialized to `0` and if no valid non-empty substring, return0. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code's `forbidden` list might contain strings that are substrings of each other and the shorter one is not a prefix of the longer? Trie handles.

Now, let's consider if the code's `isForbidden` should use `word` from outer scope and `trie` from outer scope; no `nonlocal` needed because not assigning. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code's `j` variable is assigned inside loop; no issue.

Now, let's consider if the code's `truc` variable name might be confusing but no issue.

Now, let's think about if the code's `isForbidden` should be called with `i, j` where `j` is `len(word)` and `i` is 0, but if `j` is updated to `i + truc - 1`, for next iteration `i-1`, `j` might be less than `i-1`? No, j >= i > i-1. Good.

Now, let's consider if the code's `j` update should use `i + truc` instead of `i + truc - 1`? We derived -1. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code's `isForbidden` returns `counter` where counter is l. Yes.

Now, let's think about if the code's `isForbidden` should return `counter` when "end" in t, but if the forbidden word is a prefix and there is a shorter forbidden word that is also a prefix, returns shorter. Good.

Now, let's consider if the code's `trie` insertion should mark `t["end"] = True` after the loop. If a forbidden string is empty, root end. Not relevant.

Now, let's think about if the code's `isForbidden` should use `if c not in t:` but if `t` is a node with "end" and no child for c, returns False. Good.

Now, let's consider if the code's `isForbidden` should be optimized by not using `counter` but returning `idx - start + 1` when end found. Could do:

```
for idx in range(start, end):
    c = word[idx]
    if c not in t:
        return False
    t = t[c]
    if "end" in t:
        return idx - start + 1
```

This avoids counter. But original uses counter. Either fine. If we use idx-start+1, no need counter. But keep counter for clarity.

But if `isForbidden` returns a forbidden prefix length that is not fully contained in word[i:j] due to a bug, assignment could increase j? It can't because counter <= j-i. But using min is more robust. Should we use min? It would not change correct behavior. Actually if truc > j-i, then i+truc-1 > j-1? Let's compute: truc > j-i => i+truc-1 > j-1. Since j is integer, i+truc-1 >= j. Then min(j, i+truc-1)=j. So min is safer. But isForbidden cannot return truc > j-i. Still, using min is a good defensive fix. It doesn't hurt. Should we include? It changes original assignment to min. It might be considered unnecessary but safe. Let's decide.

Now, let's consider if the code's `isForbidden` should be optimized by not using `counter` but returning `idx - start + 1` when end found. Could do:

```
for idx in range(start, end):
    c = word[idx]
    if c not in t:
        return False
    t = t[c]
    if "end" in t:
        return idx - start + 1
```

This avoids counter. But original uses counter. Either fine. If we use idx-start+1, no need counter. But keep counter for clarity.

Actually if truc > j-i, then i+truc-1 > j-1? Let's compute: truc > j-i => i+truc-1 > j-1. Since j is integer, i+truc-1 >= j. Then min(j, i+truc-1)=j. So min is safer. But isForbidden cannot return truc > j-i. Still, using min is a good defensive fix. It doesn't hurt. Should we include? It changes original assignment to min. It might be considered unnecessary but safe. Let's decide.

Actually if truc > j-i, then i+truc-1 > j-1? Let's compute: truc > j-i => i+truc-1 > j-1. Since j is integer, i+truc-1 >= j. Then min(j, i+truc-1)=j. So min is safer. But isForbidden cannot return truc > j-i. Still, using min is a good defensive fix. It doesn't hurt. Should we include? It changes original assignment to min. It might be considered unnecessary but safe. Let's decide.

I'll maybe use `j = min(j, i + truc - 1)` to be robust and express invariant. But the original code's assignment is not a bug. The prompt says fix bugs; adding min is okay.

Now, let's consider if the code's `isForbidden` should use `if t.get("end"):` instead of `if "end" in t`. If "end" is True, both. If "end" could be False? no. `in` is fine.

Now, let's think about if the code's `isForbidden` should reset `counter` to 0 for each call. It does.

Now, let's consider if the code's `isForbidden` should be defined after trie insertion. Yes.

Now, let's think about if the code's `forbidden` list might contain strings with characters that are not hashable? Strings are hashable. Good.

Now, let's consider if the code's `word` might be a list of characters? Type says str. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code's `res` should be `max(res, j - i)` or `max(res, j - i + 1)`? We determined j exclusive, length = j-i. Let's verify with simple no forbidden word "abc": j=3, i=0, res=3. If +1 would be4 wrong. Good.

Now, let's consider if the original code's `j = len(word) + 1` and `res = max(res, j - i)` maybe intended j as inclusive end? If j inclusive, length = j-i+1. But update j=i+truc-1 would length=truc, invalid. So j exclusive. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code's `isForbidden` should return the length of forbidden prefix, but if the forbidden prefix is not the shortest, could over-restrict? It returns shortest, so no.

Now, let's consider if the code's `isForbidden` should check all forbidden strings that are prefixes of `word[i:j]`, but if a forbidden string is a prefix and there is a shorter forbidden string that is also a prefix, returning shorter is correct. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code's `isForbidden` should be called with `i, j` where `j` might be `len(word)` and `i` is 0, range(0,n) includes all. Good.

Now, let's consider if the code's `j` initial `len(word)` and `i` loop descending means for i=n-1, j=n, range(n-1,n) includes last char. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code's `isForbidden` should use `for idx in range(start, end):` and if `end` is `len(word)` and `start` is `len(word)-1`, one char. Good.

Now, let's consider if the code's `isForbidden` should return `False` if `start >= end`. It would loop empty and return False. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code's `j` update should happen before `res` update because if `j` becomes i, res=0. We do.

Now, let's consider if the code's `res` should be initialized to `0` and if no valid non-empty substring, return0. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code's `forbidden` list might contain strings that are substrings of each other and the shorter one is not a prefix of the longer? Trie handles.

Now, let's consider if the code's `isForbidden` should use `word` from outer scope and `trie` from outer scope; no `nonlocal` needed because not assigning. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code's `j` variable is assigned inside loop; no issue.

Now, let's consider if the code's `truc` variable name might be confusing but no issue.

Now, let's think about if the code's `isForbidden` should be called with `i, j` where `j` is `len(word)` and `i` is 0, but if `j` is updated to `i + truc - 1`, for next iteration `i-1`, `j` might be less than `i-1`? No, j >= i > i-1. Good.

Now, let's consider if the code's `j` update should use `i + truc` instead of `i + truc - 1`? We derived -1. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code's `isForbidden` returns `counter` where counter is l. Yes.

Now, let's consider if the code's `res = max(res, j - i)` after update with j=i+l-1 gives l-1. If forbidden prefix length 1, res=0. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code's `isForbidden` should return `counter` when "end" in t, but if the forbidden word is a prefix and there is a shorter forbidden word that is also a prefix, returns shorter. Good.

Now, let's consider if the code's `trie` insertion should mark `t["end"] = True` after the loop. If a forbidden string is empty, root end. Not relevant.

Now, let's think about if the code's `isForbidden` should use `if c not in t:` but if `t` is a node with "end" and no child for c, returns False. Good.

Now, let's consider if the code's `isForbidden` should be optimized by not using `counter` but returning `idx - start + 1` when end found. Could do:

```
for idx in range(start, end):
    c = word[idx]
    if c not in t:
        return False
    t = t[c]
    if "end" in t:
        return idx - start + 1
```

This avoids counter. But original uses counter. Either fine. If we use idx-start+1, no need counter. But keep counter for clarity.

But if `isForbidden` returns a forbidden prefix length that is not fully contained in word[i:j] due to a bug, assignment could increase j? It can't because counter <= j-i. But using min is more robust. Should we use min? It would not change correct behavior. It might be slightly clearer: "j is the minimum of previous limit and the limit imposed by this forbidden prefix." But original uses assignment. If we use min, and if somehow `truc > j-i`, j would remain old j, which is correct because the forbidden prefix extends beyond current window and any r>j invalid anyway. Actually if truc > j-i, then i+truc-1 > j-1? Let's compute: truc > j-i => i+truc-1 > j-1. Since j is integer, i+truc-1 >= j. Then min(j, i+truc-1)=j. So min is safer. But isForbidden cannot return truc > j-i. Still, using min is a good defensive fix. It doesn't hurt. Should we include? It changes original assignment to min. It might be considered unnecessary but safe. Let's decide.

I'll maybe use `j = min(j, i + truc - 1)` to be robust and express invariant. But the original code's assignment is not a bug. The prompt says fix bugs; adding min is okay.

Now, let's consider if the code's `isForbidden` should be optimized by not using `counter` but returning `idx - start + 1` when end found. Could do:

```
for idx in range(start, end):
    c = word[idx]
    if c not in t:
        return False
    t = t[c]
    if "end" in t:
        return idx - start + 1
```

This avoids counter. But original uses counter. Either fine. If we use idx-start+1, no need counter. But keep counter for clarity.

Now, let's consider if the code's `isForbidden` should be optimized by not using `counter` but returning `idx - start + 1` when end found. Could do:

```
for idx in range(start, end):
    c = word[idx]
    if c not in t:
        return False
    t = t[c]
    if "end" in t:
        return idx - start + 1
```

This avoids counter. But original uses counter. Either fine. If we use idx-start+1, no need counter. But keep counter for clarity.

Actually if truc > j-i, then i+truc-1 > j-1? Let's compute: truc > j-i => i+truc-1 > j-1. Since j is integer, i+truc-1 >= j. Then min(j, i+truc-1)=j. So min is safer. But isForbidden cannot return truc > j-i. Still, using min is a good defensive fix. It doesn't hurt. Should we include? It changes original assignment to min. It might be considered unnecessary but safe. Let's decide.

Now, let's consider if the code's `j` update should be `j = min(j, i + truc - 1)` to be safe. If we use assignment, no issue. But if `isForbidden` returns a forbidden prefix length that is not fully contained in word[i:j] due to a bug, assignment could increase j? It can't because counter <= j-i. But using min is more robust. Should we use min? It would not change correct behavior. It might be slightly clearer: "j is the minimum of previous limit and the limit imposed by this forbidden prefix." But original uses assignment. If we use min, and if somehow `truc > j-i`, j would remain old j, which is correct because the forbidden prefix extends beyond current window and any r>j invalid anyway. Actually if truc > j-i, then i+truc-1 > j-1? Let's compute: truc > j-i => i+truc-1 > j-1. Since j is integer, i+truc-1 >= j. Then min(j, i+truc-1)=j. So min is safer. But isForbidden cannot return truc > j-i. Still, using min is a good defensive fix. It doesn't hurt. Should we include? It changes original assignment to min. It might be considered unnecessary but safe. Let's decide.

If we use `j = min(j, i + truc - 1)`, then in the case where `truc` is returned, since truc <= j-i, min = i+truc-1. So same. It also handles if isForbidden returns a longer prefix? no. I'll maybe use `j = min(j, i + truc - 1)` to be robust and express invariant. But the original code's assignment is not a bug. The prompt says fix bugs; adding min is okay.

However, if we use min, we need ensure `j` is not increased. Since `i+truc-1` could be > j if truc > j-i, min keeps j. Good. If truc <= j-i, min decreases. Good. No downside.

Now, let's consider if the code's `isForbidden` should be optimized by not using `counter` but returning `idx - start + 1` when end found. Could do:

```
for idx in range(start, end):
    c = word[idx]
    if c not in t:
        return False
    t = t[c]
    if "end" in t:
        return idx - start + 1
```

However, if we use min, we need ensure `j` is not increased. Since `i+truc-1` could be > j if truc > j-i, min keeps j. Good. If truc <= j-i, min decreases. Good. No downside.

Now, let's consider if the code's `isForbidden` should be optimized by not using `counter` but returning `idx - start + 1` when end found. Could do:

```
for idx in range(start, end):
    c = word[idx]
    if c not in t:
        return False
    t = t[c]
    if "end" in t:
        return idx - start + 1
```

This avoids counter. But original uses counter. Either fine. If we use idx-start+1, no need counter. But keep counter for clarity.

=== RAW ANSWER ===
```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def longestValidSubstring(self, word: str, forbidden: List[str]) -> int:
        trie = {}
        for f in forbidden:
            t = trie
            for c in f:
                if c not in t:
                    t[c] = {}
                t = t[c]
            t["end"] = True

        def isForbidden(start, end):
            t = trie
            counter = 0
            for idx in range(start, end):
                c = word[idx]
                if c not in t:
                    return False
                t = t[c]
                counter += 1
                if "end" in t:
                    return counter
            return False

        res = 0
        j = len(word)
        for i in range(len(word) - 1, -1, -1):
            truc = isForbidden(i, j)
            if truc:
                j = min(j, i + truc - 1)
            res = max(res, j - i)
        return res
```

=== FIXED CODE ===
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def longestValidSubstring(self, word: str, forbidden: List[str]) -> int:
        trie = {}
        for f in forbidden:
            t = trie
            for c in f:
                if c not in t:
                    t[c] = {}
                t = t[c]
            t["end"] = True

        def isForbidden(start, end):
            t = trie
            counter = 0
            for idx in range(start, end):
                c = word[idx]
                if c not in t:
                    return False
                t = t[c]
                counter += 1
                if "end" in t:
                    return counter
            return False

        res = 0
        j = len(word)
        for i in range(len(word) - 1, -1, -1):
            truc = isForbidden(i, j)
            if truc:
                j = min(j, i + truc - 1)
            res = max(res, j - i)
        return res
